### Why We Feel: Breaking Boundaries in Emotional Reasoning with Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07521v1)
- **Authors**: Yuxiang Lin, Jingdong Sun, Zhi-Qi Cheng, Jue Wang, Haomin Liang, Zebang Cheng, Yifei Dong, Jun-Yan He, Xiaojiang Peng, Xian-Sheng Hua
- **Abstract**: Most existing emotion analysis emphasizes which emotion arises (e.g., happy, sad, angry) but neglects the deeper why. We propose Emotion Interpretation (EI), focusing on causal factors-whether explicit (e.g., observable objects, interpersonal interactions) or implicit (e.g., cultural context, off-screen events)-that drive emotional responses. Unlike traditional emotion recognition, EI tasks require reasoning about triggers instead of mere labeling. To facilitate EI research, we present EIBench, a large-scale benchmark encompassing 1,615 basic EI samples and 50 complex EI samples featuring multifaceted emotions. Each instance demands rationale-based explanations rather than straightforward categorization. We further propose a Coarse-to-Fine Self-Ask (CFSA) annotation pipeline, which guides Vision-Language Models (VLLMs) through iterative question-answer rounds to yield high-quality labels at scale. Extensive evaluations on open-source and proprietary large language models under four experimental settings reveal consistent performance gaps-especially for more intricate scenarios-underscoring EI's potential to enrich empathetic, context-aware AI applications. Our benchmark and methods are publicly available at: https://github.com/Lum1104/EIBench, offering a foundation for advanced multimodal causal analysis and next-generation affective computing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Why We Feel: Breaking Boundaries in Emotional Reasoning with Multimodal Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new task called "Emotion Interpretation" (EI), which goes beyond simply classifying emotions to understanding *why* a person is experiencing a particular emotion. To facilitate research in this area, the authors present EIBench, a new large-scale multimodal benchmark dataset comprising both basic and complex emotional scenarios. They also propose a "Coarse-to-Fine Self-Ask" (CFSA) annotation pipeline that leverages Vision-Language Models (VLLMs) to generate high-quality annotations. The paper evaluates several open-source and proprietary large language models on EIBench, revealing performance gaps, particularly in complex scenarios, highlighting the potential of EI to improve empathetic and context-aware AI applications.  The code and dataset are publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the formal definition and introduction of the Emotion Interpretation task itself. Existing emotion recognition datasets primarily focus on labeling the emotion, whereas this paper addresses the more nuanced aspect of understanding the underlying causes. The CFSA annotation pipeline is a practical contribution, though it builds upon existing chain-of-thought prompting techniques. The dataset, EIBench, is also a significant contribution, as it fills a gap in resources available for studying causal emotion reasoning in a multimodal setting. The combination of the novel task, dataset, and annotation methodology contributes to a clear advancement.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact on the field is considerable. If EI can be successfully tackled by AI systems, it could lead to more empathetic and context-aware applications in areas like mental health support, customer service, and human-computer interaction. By focusing on *why* rather than simply *what*, the paper opens up new avenues for research in affective computing. The EIBench dataset provides a valuable resource for the community to develop and evaluate models for EI. The experiments with LLMs, even if they reveal performance gaps, provide a crucial baseline and insights for future research directions.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing emotion recognition approaches and the need for EI.
    *   **Comprehensive Dataset:** EIBench is a well-designed, large-scale dataset with both basic and complex scenarios.
    *   **Practical Annotation Pipeline:** CFSA offers a practical approach to annotating data for EI tasks.
    *   **Thorough Evaluation:** The paper evaluates a wide range of LLMs under different experimental settings.
    *   **Public Availability:** The public release of both the dataset and methods increases reproducibility and promotes further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on VLLMs:** The CFSA pipeline heavily relies on the capabilities of VLLMs, which may introduce biases or limitations. While human-in-the-loop cleaning is implemented, some subtle nuances may still be missed.
    *   **Limited Generalizability of Annotations:** Although annotation quality checks are in place, the inherent subjectivity of emotional interpretation may lead to variations in annotations, affecting generalizability.
    *   **Metrics:**  While the chosen metrics, especially Emotional Trigger Recall, is reasonable, the long-term coherence is insufficient to capture the full quality of emotional understanding.  A metric more closely tied to causal understanding could be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in affective computing, multimodal learning, and explainable AI. The EIBench dataset could become a standard benchmark for evaluating EI models. The CFSA annotation pipeline could inspire new methods for data collection and annotation in related areas.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** While the paper presents a strong case for the importance of EI, the reliance on VLLMs for data generation and the potential for subjective bias in annotation, combined with moderately improved performance with complex settings, hold it back from being a truly exceptional contribution. The work provides a solid foundation, but further refinement of both the annotation process and evaluation metrics is needed to fully realize the potential of EI.

### Supervised Optimism Correction: Be Confident When LLMs Are Sure
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07527v1)
- **Authors**: Junjie Zhang, Rushuai Yang, Shunyu Liu, Ting-En Lin, Fei Huang, Yi Chen, Yongbin Li, Dacheng Tao
- **Abstract**: In this work, we establish a novel theoretical connection between supervised fine-tuning and offline reinforcement learning under the token-level Markov decision process, revealing that large language models indeed learn an implicit $Q$-function for inference. Through this theoretical lens, we demonstrate that the widely used beam search method suffers from unacceptable over-optimism, where inference errors are inevitably amplified due to inflated $Q$-value estimations of suboptimal steps. To address this limitation, we propose Supervised Optimism Correction(SOC), which introduces a simple yet effective auxiliary loss for token-level $Q$-value estimations during supervised fine-tuning. Specifically, the auxiliary loss employs implicit value regularization to boost model confidence in expert-demonstrated responses, thereby suppressing over-optimism toward insufficiently supervised responses. Extensive experiments on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, including GSM8K, MATH, and GAOKAO, showcase the superiority of the proposed SOC with beam search across a series of open-source models.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary of the paper followed by a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the over-optimism problem in beam search when applied to Large Language Models (LLMs) fine-tuned using supervised fine-tuning (SFT). It theoretically demonstrates that LLMs learn an implicit Q-function during SFT and that beam search often selects sequences with inflated Q-value estimations, leading to suboptimal results.  To counter this, the authors propose Supervised Optimism Correction (SOC), an auxiliary loss that boosts model confidence in expert-demonstrated responses, suppressing over-optimism toward insufficiently supervised responses.  Experimental results on mathematical reasoning benchmarks show SOC improves performance in models like Qwen across various beam widths.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The theoretical connection between SFT and offline RL through the lens of a Q-function is a valuable contribution. Formally defining the over-optimism problem in the context of LLMs and beam search is also novel. While the idea of adding regularization terms is not entirely new, applying it specifically to address this over-optimism in SFT and beam search for LLMs is a relatively original approach.

*   **Significance:** The problem of over-optimism in beam search is practically relevant. LLMs are often used with beam search, and addressing its limitations can lead to better performance, particularly for complex reasoning tasks. If the improvement translates to other tasks or larger models, it would be more significant. The SOC method is simple to implement and doesn't require architectural changes, making it readily adoptable.  The improved performance on GSM8K, MATH, and GAOKAO suggests a practical benefit.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong theoretical justification.
    *   Clear problem definition and proposed solution.
    *   Easy to implement and does not require architectural change.
    *   Demonstrated improvement on multiple benchmark datasets.
    *   Ablation studies to investigate the effect of various factors.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Experiments are primarily focused on mathematical reasoning.  It's unclear how well SOC would generalize to other tasks or domains.
    *   The improvement, while notable, might not be considered groundbreaking in all cases. Further exploration of the scaling benefits of SOC for even larger models would strengthen the work.
    *   The theoretical section, while providing a solid foundation, relies on assumptions that may not perfectly hold in real-world LLM scenarios (e.g., deterministic transitions).

*   **Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence how LLMs are trained and used with beam search. It provides a new perspective on SFT and decoding, and the proposed SOC method could become a standard technique for mitigating over-optimism in reasoning tasks. It also opens up avenues for further research, such as exploring other regularization techniques or adapting SOC for different decoding strategies.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The paper combines theoretical insight with empirical validation. The theoretical model provides a framework for understanding why beam search suffers from over-optimism, and the experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of SOC. However, the paper is somewhat limited by its focus on mathematical reasoning and relatively small parameter models. The simplicity of the method and its clear presentation add to the potential for the proposed method to be adapted by other researchers.

Score: 7

**Justification:**

The paper is a well-executed piece of research with clear theoretical justification and solid empirical results.  Its novelty is sufficient, particularly in connecting SFT and offline RL to reveal the implicit Q-function learning and the over-optimism problem in beam search. The SOC method is simple yet effective. However, the relatively narrow scope of the experiments (primarily mathematical reasoning) and the incremental nature of the improvement justify a slightly lower score than would be appropriate if it demonstrated a wider, more impactful effect. A slightly stronger claim could have been made regarding the generalizability of the work if further experiments were included covering other tasks. Overall, it contributes meaningfully to the understanding and improvement of LLMs and beam search.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Automating the Path: An R&D Agenda for Human-Centered AI and Visualization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07529v1)
- **Authors**: Niklas Elmqvist, Clemens Nylandsted Klokmose
- **Abstract**: The emergence of generative AI, large language models (LLMs), and foundation models is fundamentally reshaping computer science, and visualization and visual analytics are no exception. We present a systematic framework for understanding how human-centered AI (HCAI) can transform the visualization discipline. Our framework maps four key HCAI tool capabilities -- amplify, augment, empower, and enhance -- onto the four phases of visual sensemaking: view, explore, schematize, and report. For each combination, we review existing tools, envision future possibilities, identify challenges and pitfalls, and examine ethical considerations. This design space can serve as an R\&D agenda for both visualization researchers and practitioners to integrate AI into their work as well as understanding how visualization can support HCAI research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Automating the Path: An R&D Agenda for Human-Centered AI and Visualization":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the transformative impact of generative AI, large language models (LLMs), and foundation models on visualization and visual analytics.  It proposes a framework to understand how human-centered AI (HCAI) can be integrated into the visualization discipline. This framework maps four HCAI capabilities (amplify, augment, empower, enhance) onto the four phases of visual sensemaking (prepare, explore, schematize, report).  For each combination, the authors review existing tools, envision future possibilities, identify challenges and potential pitfalls, and examine ethical considerations, providing an R&D agenda for integrating AI into visualization while emphasizing its role in HCAI research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Timely and Relevant:** The paper addresses a critical and rapidly evolving area: the intersection of AI and visualization. The timing is excellent, given the recent surge in AI capabilities and their potential impact on how visual analytics is performed.
    * **Comprehensive Framework:** The proposed framework of mapping HCAI capabilities onto the phases of visual sensemaking provides a structured and useful way to think about how AI can be integrated.  The explicit consideration of ethical issues, challenges, and pitfalls is a significant strength, as these are often overlooked in discussions about AI adoption.
    * **Balanced Perspective:** The paper avoids the pitfall of simply advocating for AI as a replacement for human analysts.  Instead, it emphasizes the augmentation and empowerment of analysts, maintaining a human-centered approach.
    * **Practical Examples:**  The inclusion of existing HCAI tools, like Stanford Wrangler and DataSite, grounds the discussion and provides concrete examples of how the framework can be applied.
    * **Addresses Potential Downsides:** The paper critically addresses potential downsides, such as data loss, conversion errors, biases, and loss of agency, demonstrating a nuanced understanding of the complexities of integrating AI.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Novelty of Framework:** While the framework is well-structured, the combination of HCAI principles with the sensemaking pipeline is not entirely novel. Earlier work has touched on similar ideas; the paper's contribution lies in the structured presentation and comprehensive analysis.
    * **Somewhat Speculative:** The "future possibilities" section, while valuable, is inherently speculative.  It's difficult to predict exactly how AI will evolve and impact visualization in the coming years.
    * **Depth of Analysis:** In some sections, the analysis could be deeper. While the paper covers a broad range of topics, it sometimes lacks detailed discussion of specific AI algorithms or visualization techniques. This is perhaps an inevitable consequence of the paper's scope.
    * **Limited Evaluation of Tools:** The paper highlights example tools but does not provide any empirical evaluation data demonstrating the effectiveness of these tools or the proposed framework. The framework could be strengthened by showing practical benefits through experimental results.

* **Significance:** The paper is significant because it provides a roadmap for visualization researchers and practitioners to navigate the rapidly changing landscape of AI. It promotes a responsible and human-centered approach to AI integration, highlighting both the opportunities and challenges. It also points out the equally important role of visualization for advancing AI. The framework offers a valuable starting point for future research and development in this area.

**Overall Justification for Score:**

This paper provides a timely, relevant, and structured framework for incorporating AI into the visualization field, particularly through the lens of human-centered AI. The identification of challenges and ethical considerations strengthens its contribution. While the framework's core ideas are not entirely novel, the comprehensive mapping of HCAI capabilities to visual sensemaking phases and the emphasis on human agency elevate its significance. The speculative nature of some of the "future possibilities" and the lack of empirical validation are minor drawbacks.  The overall impact of the paper lies in providing a clear and thought-provoking agenda for future research and development.

### A taxonomy of epistemic injustice in the context of AI and the case for generative hermeneutical erasure
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07531v1)
- **Authors**: Warmhold Jan Thomas Mollema
- **Abstract**: Whether related to machine learning models' epistemic opacity, algorithmic classification systems' discriminatory automation of testimonial prejudice, the distortion of human beliefs via the 'hallucinations' of generative AI, the inclusion of the global South in global AI governance, the execution of bureaucratic violence via algorithmic systems, or located in the interaction with conversational artificial agents epistemic injustice related to AI is a growing concern. Based on a proposed general taxonomy of epistemic injustice, this paper first sketches a taxonomy of the types of epistemic injustice in the context of AI, relying on the work of scholars from the fields of philosophy of technology, political philosophy and social epistemology. Secondly, an additional perspective on epistemic injustice in the context of AI: generative hermeneutical erasure. I argue that this injustice that can come about through the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) and contend that generative AI, when being deployed outside of its Western space of conception, can have effects of conceptual erasure, particularly in the epistemic domain, followed by forms of conceptual disruption caused by a mismatch between AI system and the interlocutor in terms of conceptual frameworks. AI systems' 'view from nowhere' epistemically inferiorizes non-Western epistemologies and thereby contributes to the erosion of their epistemic particulars, gradually contributing to hermeneutical erasure. This work's relevance lies in proposal of a taxonomy that allows epistemic injustices to be mapped in the AI domain and the proposal of a novel form of AI-related epistemic injustice.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary of the paper and a rigorous critical evaluation, including a score and justification.

**Summary:**

The paper "A taxonomy of epistemic injustice in the context of AI and the case for generative hermeneutical erasure" proposes a taxonomy of epistemic injustices related to the development and deployment of artificial intelligence (AI) systems. Building on existing work in philosophy of technology, political philosophy, and social epistemology (particularly Miranda Fricker's framework), the author first outlines a general taxonomy of epistemic injustice.  This taxonomy includes: Participatory Injustice, Contributory Injustice, Epistemic Labor Invalidation, Epistemic Domination/Exploitation, Testimonial Injustice, Hermeneutical Injustice, Zetetic Injustice, and Testimonial Spurning. Then, the paper extends this framework to the context of AI, examining how these forms of injustice manifest in algorithmic systems. Further, the author introduces a novel category specific to AI: generative hermeneutical erasure. This occurs especially in intercultural AI system deployment where AI, trained on Western data, "erases" or disrupts non-Western epistemologies, hindering socioculturally collective ways of making sense of the world. The paper concludes with a discussion of potential remedies for algorithmic epistemic injustice, including the decolonization of AI.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Taxonomy:** The paper's primary strength lies in its attempt to systematically categorize epistemic injustices within the AI domain. By building upon existing philosophical frameworks, the taxonomy provides a structured approach to understanding a complex and multifaceted issue.
*   **Novel Category of Generative Hermeneutical Erasure:** The introduction of "generative hermeneutical erasure" is a valuable contribution. It highlights a subtle but significant problem: the potential for AI, particularly generative models, to erode or displace non-dominant epistemologies through a process akin to epistemic colonization.  The link to Wittgensteinian hinge epistemology offers a compelling analytical lens.
*   **Emphasis on Intercultural Context:** The paper's focus on the intercultural deployment of AI and its potential to exacerbate existing power imbalances is crucial. It moves beyond discussions of bias in data sets to consider the broader implications of AI for epistemic diversity and autonomy.
*   **Engagement with Existing Literature:** The paper demonstrates a strong command of the relevant literature in philosophy, technology studies, and AI ethics. It effectively synthesizes diverse perspectives and builds upon them.
*   **Policy Implications:** By identifying specific types of epistemic injustice in the AI domain, the paper lays the groundwork for more targeted and effective policy interventions. The emphasis on decolonizing AI also highlights the need for a more inclusive and equitable approach to AI development.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Speculative Nature of Generative Hermeneutical Erasure:** While the concept of generative hermeneutical erasure is novel and insightful, it remains somewhat speculative. The paper could benefit from more concrete examples and empirical evidence to support the claim that AI systems are actively "erasing" non-Western epistemologies. The provided example involving AI not recognizing Ai Weiwei is interesting, but doesn't fully capture the complexity of the claimed phenomena.
*   **Limited Empirical Engagement:** While the paper references existing literature, it lacks primary empirical research to support its claims about the impact of AI on epistemic justice. More case studies and real-world examples would strengthen the analysis.
*   **Depth of Discussion on Remedies:** While the paper acknowledges the need for remedies and proposes some initial strategies, the discussion could be more developed. Exploring concrete policy interventions and design solutions in greater detail would enhance the paper's practical relevance.
*   **Risk of Oversimplification:** Although the author acknowledges the risk of reducing epistemic injustices to abstract concepts, the sheer number of categories in the taxonomy creates the potential for oversimplification. It could be beneficial to focus on a subset of categories and explore them in greater depth. The strict mono-inheritance approach also feels somewhat artificial.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper is novel in its attempt to provide a comprehensive taxonomy of epistemic injustices in the context of AI, specifically with the concept of generative hermeneutical erasure. The concept is a valuable addition to the conversation. However, the taxonomy could be more focused and empirically grounded. It has the potential to influence AI ethics, policy, and design by raising awareness of subtle but significant power imbalances. The exploration of how AI's biases can disrupt and overwrite local epistemologies is a very important and timely concern.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper has clear merit, but also some tangible areas for improvement. Its systematic approach is laudable. However, the lack of deep empirical grounding, particularly for the most novel claim, limits its current impact.

### STeP: A General and Scalable Framework for Solving Video Inverse Problems with Spatiotemporal Diffusion Priors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07549v1)
- **Authors**: Bingliang Zhang, Zihui Wu, Berthy T. Feng, Yang Song, Yisong Yue, Katherine L. Bouman
- **Abstract**: We study how to solve general Bayesian inverse problems involving videos using diffusion model priors. While it is desirable to use a video diffusion prior to effectively capture complex temporal relationships, due to the computational and data requirements of training such a model, prior work has instead relied on image diffusion priors on single frames combined with heuristics to enforce temporal consistency. However, these approaches struggle with faithfully recovering the underlying temporal relationships, particularly for tasks with high temporal uncertainty. In this paper, we demonstrate the feasibility of practical and accessible spatiotemporal diffusion priors by fine-tuning latent video diffusion models from pretrained image diffusion models using limited videos in specific domains. Leveraging this plug-and-play spatiotemporal diffusion prior, we introduce a general and scalable framework for solving video inverse problems. We then apply our framework to two challenging scientific video inverse problems--black hole imaging and dynamic MRI. Our framework enables the generation of diverse, high-fidelity video reconstructions that not only fit observations but also recover multi-modal solutions. By incorporating a spatiotemporal diffusion prior, we significantly improve our ability to capture complex temporal relationships in the data while also enhancing spatial fidelity.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces STEP, a general and scalable framework for solving video inverse problems using spatiotemporal diffusion priors. Addressing the limitations of existing methods that rely on image diffusion priors and heuristics to enforce temporal consistency, STEP fine-tunes latent video diffusion models from pre-trained image diffusion models using limited video data. This data-efficient approach drastically reduces training costs. The framework is applied to black hole imaging and dynamic MRI, demonstrating its ability to generate diverse, high-fidelity video reconstructions that both fit observations and capture multi-modal solutions, improving temporal and spatial consistency.  The authors leverage a state-of-the-art plug-and-play diffusion solver (DAPS) to combine their spatiotemporal diffusion prior with knowledge of the inverse problem.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The main novelty lies in the *efficient* training of a *spatiotemporal* diffusion prior for video inverse problems. While using diffusion models for inverse problems is not new, and neither is using diffusion models for videos, the specific method of *fine-tuning a latent video diffusion model from a pre-trained image diffusion model using limited video data* is the key contribution.  It overcomes the significant data and computational requirements typically associated with training video diffusion models from scratch. The integration into a PnP framework like DAPS, while effective, is less novel than the diffusion prior training method itself. The introduction of d-PSNR and d-SSIM, while useful metrics, do not represent substantial methodological novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the practical applicability of the method to scientific domains where video data is scarce but related image data is abundant. Overcoming the data bottleneck for video diffusion models opens the door to better solutions for inverse problems in fields like astronomy and medical imaging. The demonstrable improvement in temporal consistency, as measured by d-PSNR, is a strong selling point. The ability to capture multi-modal solutions is also important for scientific applications where uncertainty quantification is crucial. The experimental results, showcasing performance on real-world scientific problems, enhance the paper's practical impact.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Data-efficient training method for video diffusion priors.
    *   Demonstrated improvement in temporal consistency (high d-PSNR).
    *   Capability of capturing multi-modal solutions.
    *   Application to challenging scientific inverse problems (black hole imaging and dynamic MRI).
    *   Well-written and clear presentation of the method and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The novelty is primarily concentrated on the diffusion prior training aspect.  The PnP framework is relatively standard.
    *   While the results are impressive, a deeper analysis of *why* this fine-tuning approach works so well would further strengthen the paper.  A theoretical justification or a more detailed analysis of the learned features could provide more insight.
    *   The computation cost is still high.
    *   Lack of comparison with some video restoration papers (if any exists in the selected area) is a point to be concerned.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in video inverse problems, particularly in scientific domains. The data-efficient training strategy could be adopted by other researchers working with limited video datasets. The framework's ability to capture multi-modal solutions may also inspire further work on uncertainty quantification in scientific imaging. Further research will be needed to determine if this method can be generalized to other video inverse problems outside the demonstrated applications.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution by addressing a key challenge in video diffusion models – the high data and computational demands.  The presented fine-tuning approach for spatiotemporal diffusion priors is practical and yields tangible improvements, particularly in temporal consistency, demonstrated in meaningful applications. The main limitation is that the algorithmic framework is fairly standard. Given the clear benefits and potential for impact within specific application areas, the assigned score reflects the positive aspects and the limitations of the work.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Using LLMs for Analyzing AIS Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07557v1)
- **Authors**: Gaspard Mertends, Gilles Dejaegere, Mahmoud Sakr
- **Abstract**: Recent research in Large Language Models (LLMs), has had a profound impact across various fields, including mobility data science. This paper explores the and experiment with different approaches to using LLMs for analyzing AIS data. We propose a set of carefully designed queries to assess the reasoning capabilities of LLMs in this kind of tasks. Further, we experiment with four different methods: (1) using LLMs as a natural language interface to a spatial database, (2) reasoning on raw data, (3) reasoning on compressed trajectories, and (4) reasoning on semantic trajectories. We investigate the strengths and weaknesses for the four methods, and discuss the findings. The goal is to provide valuable insights for both researchers and practitioners on selecting the most appropriate LLM-based method depending on their specific data analysis objectives.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for analyzing Automatic Identification System (AIS) data, which tracks maritime vessel movements. It investigates four different methods: 1) using LLMs as a Natural Language Interface to Databases (NLIDB) with a PostGIS database, 2) zero-shot data analysis (ZSA) on raw AIS data, 3) ZSA on compressed AIS trajectories, and 4) ZSA on semantically enriched AIS trajectories. The study proposes a taxonomy of 27 maritime queries across four categories (Attribute, Individual Trajectory, Interaction, and Data-fusion) to assess the reasoning capabilities of the LLMs. The authors conduct experiments using Gemini-1.5-flash and evaluate the accuracy of each method.  The results indicate that while NLIDB offers stable performance, zero-shot approaches show varying levels of effectiveness depending on the query and dataset size.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its direct comparison of different LLM-based approaches for AIS data analysis *without* heavy reliance on traditional GIS tools as intermediaries.  While other works use LLMs to generate SQL queries or focus on specific tasks like trajectory prediction, this paper attempts a broader, more direct reasoning capability assessment. The taxonomy of maritime queries is a useful contribution, as it goes beyond simple range queries and attempts to capture more complex analytical tasks. However, the individual components are not particularly novel; NLIDB and ZSA are well-established techniques, and the trajectory compression method is also known.  The combination and evaluation in this specific domain represent the main contribution.

*   **Significance:** The work has the potential to be significant for researchers and practitioners in maritime data analysis and geospatial AI. The comparison of different LLM-based approaches offers practical insights for selecting appropriate methods based on specific objectives. The paper highlights the strengths and weaknesses of each approach, guiding future research towards more effective LLM-powered AIS data analysis.  Furthermore, the taxonomy of maritime queries provides a benchmark for evaluating LLM performance on this type of data. The observation that raw data can perform well, without any form of pre-treatment, needs further investigation and will have big implications if it can be proven robust. The inclusion of semantic trajectories, while not showing strong improvement in this study, is an important direction for future investigation as it could bridge the gap between raw data and human understanding.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined methodology with a clear experimental setup.
    *   Comprehensive taxonomy of maritime queries, which is a valuable resource.
    *   Detailed comparison of different LLM-based approaches.
    *   Discussion of practical considerations such as context window size and temperature parameter settings.
    *   Explicitly mentions the "blind trust" issue with LLMs, showing awareness of ethical implications.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited number of ships/trajectories in the dataset (300 ships) which might not fully represent the complexity of real-world AIS data.
    *   Only one LLM (Gemini-1.5-flash) was used, limiting the generalizability of the results.
    *   The use of PostGIS in the NLIDB system might not be ideal, and a different spatial database could potentially improve performance. It's unclear how optimized the PostGIS queries were, or if this represents a realistic baseline.
    *   Semantic trajectory conversion relies on OSM data quality and the definition of geographical areas, which may introduce bias.
    *   The limited improvement from trajectory simplification and semantic enrichment might indicate limitations with the simplification/enrichment process itself.  More sophisticated techniques could be explored.
    *   The experiments have only been evaluated using metrics that rely on correct/incorrect answers, and do not assess the uncertainty in the results that LLMs might output.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to influence the adoption of LLMs in maritime data analysis. The findings can inform the development of more effective LLM-based tools and techniques. The paper's taxonomy and experimental results provide a basis for future research and benchmarking efforts.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by providing a systematic comparison of different LLM-based approaches for AIS data analysis. The taxonomy of maritime queries and the practical insights offered are particularly noteworthy. However, the limitations in dataset size, LLM selection, and some of the data pre-processing techniques prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work. The novelty is incremental rather than revolutionary, as it primarily combines existing techniques in a specific domain. Therefore, a score that reflects a solid but not exceptional contribution is appropriate.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### PhaseGen: A Diffusion-Based Approach for Complex-Valued MRI Data Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07560v1)
- **Authors**: Moritz Rempe, Fabian Hörst, Helmut Becker, Marco Schlimbach, Lukas Rotkopf, Kevin Kröninger, Jens Kleesiek
- **Abstract**: Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) raw data, or k-Space data, is complex-valued, containing both magnitude and phase information. However, clinical and existing Artificial Intelligence (AI)-based methods focus only on magnitude images, discarding the phase data despite its potential for downstream tasks, such as tumor segmentation and classification. In this work, we introduce $\textit{PhaseGen}$, a novel complex-valued diffusion model for generating synthetic MRI raw data conditioned on magnitude images, commonly used in clinical practice. This enables the creation of artificial complex-valued raw data, allowing pretraining for models that require k-Space information. We evaluate PhaseGen on two tasks: skull-stripping directly in k-Space and MRI reconstruction using the publicly available FastMRI dataset. Our results show that training with synthetic phase data significantly improves generalization for skull-stripping on real-world data, with an increased segmentation accuracy from $41.1\%$ to $80.1\%$, and enhances MRI reconstruction when combined with limited real-world data. This work presents a step forward in utilizing generative AI to bridge the gap between magnitude-based datasets and the complex-valued nature of MRI raw data. This approach allows researchers to leverage the vast amount of avaliable image domain data in combination with the information-rich k-Space data for more accurate and efficient diagnostic tasks. We make our code publicly $\href{https://github.com/TIO-IKIM/PhaseGen}{\text{available here}}$.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "PhaseGen," a novel complex-valued diffusion model designed to generate synthetic MRI raw data (k-Space data) conditioned on magnitude images. The primary motivation is to bridge the gap between the abundance of magnitude-based MRI datasets and the scarcity of complex-valued k-Space data, which contains valuable phase information often discarded in clinical and AI-based analyses. PhaseGen aims to enable researchers to leverage magnitude image domain data for pretraining models that require k-Space information.  The authors evaluate PhaseGen on two tasks: skull stripping directly in k-Space and MRI reconstruction. Results suggest that training with synthetic phase data improves generalization for skull stripping on real data and enhances MRI reconstruction when combined with limited real-world data. The code is made publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core contribution, using a diffusion model to generate complex-valued k-space data conditioned on magnitude images, appears relatively novel. While diffusion models have been applied to MRI reconstruction and image generation, their application to directly generating synthetic *complex-valued* raw data, especially with the stated purpose of expanding pre-training capabilities, seems to be a unique contribution. The complex-valued diffusion process itself is also a non-trivial adaptation of existing methods.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance lies in addressing a clear problem: the limited availability of complex-valued MRI data hinders the development of AI models that could benefit from phase information. PhaseGen offers a way to create synthetic k-Space data, potentially unlocking the use of vast magnitude image datasets for tasks that previously required scarce raw data. The improvement in skull-stripping generalization and MRI reconstruction, although modest, validates this potential.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies a bottleneck in MRI research and offers a targeted solution.
    *   **Technical Soundness:** The complex-valued diffusion model appears well-designed and implemented.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments, while focused on two specific tasks, provide evidence of the model's effectiveness.
    *   **Publicly Available Code:**  This increases the reproducibility and usability of the research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Evaluation:** The validation is restricted to skull stripping and MRI reconstruction. While these are relevant, more diverse tasks (e.g., pathology detection, image registration, quantitative MRI) could strengthen the claims of general applicability.
    *   **Modest Improvement:** While the results show improvement, especially for skull stripping, it's important to acknowledge that the improvements in reconstruction are not dramatic when comparing to using real raw data. The synthetic data may not perfectly capture the complexities of real k-Space data.
    *   **Generalizability Assumptions:** The paper assumes that using *any* plausible phase data will improve performance. While this is supported by the skull-stripping results, the quality and realism of the generated phase is crucial.
    *   **Coil Sensitivity Limitation:** The model doesn't account for coil sensitivity, a significant factor in MRI acquisition. This might limit the quality and realism of the generated data, particularly for multi-coil acquisitions.
    *   **Lack of Comparison with Domain Adaptation Techniques:** The performance increases observed for downstream tasks might also have been achievable via well-established domain adaptation methods. A direct comparison with such alternatives would strengthen the case for the new approach proposed.
    *   **No human interpretability test for the synthetic k-space data:** It is unclear if the quality of k-space data can be evaluated using image-quality metrics alone. In many applications, downstream AI models may also be evaluated using human-factors metrics as well.

*   **Potential Influence:**  PhaseGen could facilitate research into new AI-driven MRI applications that leverage phase information. By making k-Space pre-training more accessible, it could accelerate progress in tasks like quantitative MRI and advanced image analysis.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper has elements of novelty, significance and clear practical value. However, the weaknesses in terms of validation and limitations discussed above prevent a very high score. Therefore, a moderate score appears most appropriate.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Diffusion Transformers for Tabular Data Time Series Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07566v1)
- **Authors**: Fabrizio Garuti, Enver Sangineto, Simone Luetto, Lorenzo Forni, Rita Cucchiara
- **Abstract**: Tabular data generation has recently attracted a growing interest due to its different application scenarios. However, generating time series of tabular data, where each element of the series depends on the others, remains a largely unexplored domain. This gap is probably due to the difficulty of jointly solving different problems, the main of which are the heterogeneity of tabular data (a problem common to non-time-dependent approaches) and the variable length of a time series. In this paper, we propose a Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) based approach for tabular data series generation. Inspired by the recent success of DiTs in image and video generation, we extend this framework to deal with heterogeneous data and variable-length sequences. Using extensive experiments on six datasets, we show that the proposed approach outperforms previous work by a large margin.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TabDiT, a novel approach for generating time series of tabular data. It tackles the challenges of heterogeneity in tabular data and the variable length of time series by adapting Diffusion Transformers (DiTs), which are typically used in image and video generation.  TabDiT uses a Latent Diffusion Model (LDM) framework.  It differs from standard LDMs by decomposing the representation problem; instead of a holistic VAE for the entire time series, it uses a non-holistic VAE that compresses individual tabular rows.  A DiT-based denoising network then combines these latent embeddings, modeling the temporal dependencies.  The paper also proposes an AR VAE decoder for better intra-row consistency and a variable-range decimal representation for numerical fields.  The method includes explicit padding prediction for handling variable-length series.  Experiments on six datasets demonstrate that TabDiT outperforms previous methods in both conditional and unconditional generation scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in adapting the Diffusion Transformer architecture to the specific challenges of tabular data time series generation.  Existing diffusion models for tabular data primarily focus on single-row generation and struggle with long, variable-length sequences. The core contributions are: 1) The decomposition of the latent space using a non-holistic VAE to manage the complexity and variability of tabular time series. 2) A specific representation learning method for numerical data and 3) The explicit modeling of the padding to determine the time series length. While diffusion models and transformers are not new in themselves, their specific combination and adaptation within this tabular context, especially the non-holistic VAE component, constitute a significant innovative step.

*   **Significance:** The ability to generate realistic and diverse tabular data time series has significant implications for several applications, including privacy preservation (synthesizing data for public release), data augmentation, and fairness promotion. The paper demonstrates TabDiT's effectiveness across a range of datasets, suggesting its broad applicability. The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing a relatively unexplored area of generative AI, and the promising results can spur further research and development in this area. The paper also proposes a new metric that can serve as a standard to evaluate this research area and facilitates future comparisons.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-explained methodology, including the rationale behind design choices.
    *   Extensive experiments on a diverse set of datasets.
    *   Thorough ablation study to demonstrate the importance of individual components.
    *   Significant performance improvements over baselines.
    *   The paper proposes a novel metric to evaluate the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited comparison to existing methods, primarily due to the scarcity of relevant research with available code. The baselines are often variations of the authors' architecture.
    *   While the paper mentions the advantages of explicit padding prediction, a more in-depth analysis of its impact on downstream tasks would be beneficial.
    *   The specific hyperparameter choices are discussed (Sec. D.3), but they are not compared extensively in an ablation study.
    *   Some limitations are discussed in the supplementary, including the difficulty in using REaLTabFormer in a larger scale.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   TabDiT's modular architecture and effective handling of heterogeneous data could inspire new generative models for other complex data types.
    *   The proposed evaluation metric could become a standard for assessing tabular data time series generators, facilitating fair comparisons across methods.
    *   The approach may stimulate more research into adapting diffusion models to the unique challenges of tabular data.

*   **Justification for Score:**

While the paper exhibits excellent methodology and empirical results, the lack of strong comparison benchmarks due to the nascent state of the field limits its overall significance at this stage. The technique is novel and the ablations clearly show how the parts interact together. The thorough experiments in a field where there is no standard metric is an additional strength.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Exploring Human-Like Thinking in Search Simulations with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07570v1)
- **Authors**: Erhan Zhang, Xingzhu Wang, Peiyuan Gong, Zixuan Yang, Jiaxin Mao
- **Abstract**: Simulating user search behavior is a critical task in information retrieval, which can be employed for user behavior modeling, data augmentation, and system evaluation. Recent advancements in large language models (LLMs) have opened up new possibilities for generating human-like actions including querying, browsing, and clicking. In this work, we explore the integration of human-like thinking into search simulations by leveraging LLMs to simulate users' hidden cognitive processes. Specifically, given a search task and context, we prompt LLMs to first think like a human before executing the corresponding action. As existing search datasets do not include users' thought processes, we conducted a user study to collect a new dataset enriched with users' explicit thinking. We investigate the impact of incorporating such human-like thinking on simulation performance and apply supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to teach LLMs to emulate both human thinking and actions. Our experiments span two dimensions in leveraging LLMs for user simulation: (1) with or without explicit thinking, and (2) with or without fine-tuning on the thinking-augmented dataset. The results demonstrate the feasibility and potential of incorporating human-like thinking in user simulations, though performance improvements on some metrics remain modest. We believe this exploration provides new avenues and inspirations for advancing user behavior modeling in search simulations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

This paper explores the integration of human-like thinking into user search simulations using large language models (LLMs). The authors hypothesize that incorporating explicit cognitive processes into the simulation, rather than just focusing on observable actions, will improve the realism and interpretability of the simulated user behavior.  They conducted a user study to collect a dataset augmented with users' verbalized thoughts (think-aloud protocol) during search tasks.  They then fine-tuned LLMs to emulate both user actions and these explicit thoughts.  The performance of the simulated agents was evaluated with and without explicit thinking incorporated, and with and without fine-tuning on the new dataset.  The results suggest the feasibility of incorporating human-like thinking, but also show that performance improvements are somewhat modest and that the models need further refinement to better emulate human search behaviour.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of incorporating human-like thinking into user simulations is not entirely new; latent variables and cognitive-aware models have been explored before. However, this paper is novel in its explicit attempt to capture and integrate *verbalized* thought processes directly into the simulation loop using LLMs. This "think-aloud" approach directly prompts LLMs to simulate the human thought processes. The collection of a dedicated think-aloud dataset for search simulation is also a significant contribution.

*   **Significance:** Simulating realistic user behavior is critical for information retrieval research. It enables system evaluation, data augmentation, and the development of more effective search algorithms. The incorporation of cognitive factors could lead to more accurate and insightful simulations, potentially improving the design and evaluation of future search systems. Furthermore, it shows initial evidence that the thought processes behind decision-making are also capable of being transferred to downstream LLMs to make better decisions.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Dataset Collection:** The creation of a dataset with user think-aloud data is a major strength. Existing search datasets lack this kind of cognitive information, making this contribution valuable to the community.
    *   **Explicit Integration of Thoughts:** The paper directly addresses the "thinking behind actions," attempting to model the generative process of thought and actions.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experimental setup is fairly thorough, covering different LLMs, fine-tuning scenarios, and evaluation metrics.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Modest Performance Improvements:** The performance improvements from incorporating human-like thinking are not as substantial as one might expect. This could indicate that current LLMs are not yet fully capable of capturing the nuances of human cognitive processes in search. The modest improvements suggest potential limitations in the methodology and dataset, requiring further refinement.
    *   **Lack of Deeper Cognitive Modeling:** While the paper incorporates "thinking," it primarily does so at a surface level. It doesn't delve into deeper cognitive models of memory, goals, or information processing. In other words, it models the "what" but not necessarily the "why".
    *   **Limited Scope:** The study is confined to a relatively controlled lab environment, which may not fully capture the complexities of real-world search behavior.
    *   **Data Statistics:** the thoughts-actions and observation statistics are quite closely correlated, which might indicate a relatively small number of users, each conducting a search and thought action.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to stimulate further research into cognitive user modeling with LLMs. The dataset and methodology provide a foundation for others to build upon. The paper's findings highlight the challenges and opportunities in this area, potentially leading to more sophisticated and realistic user simulations in the future.

*   **Justification:** The paper provides a valuable dataset and explores a novel approach to user simulation. However, the relatively modest performance improvements and limitations in cognitive modeling prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution. The value of the paper mostly stems from it being one of the first to explore this approach and make a data contribution.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:** While the idea and data collection are novel and valuable, the limited performance gain tempers the overall impact. The current implementation, while sound, seems to just scratch the surface of what's possible when we model user cognition. It lays the groundwork for future, more in-depth explorations of cognitive user simulation.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 6/10

### REANIMATOR: Reanimate Retrieval Test Collections with Extracted and Synthetic Resources
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07584v1)
- **Authors**: Björn Engelmann, Fabian Haak, Philipp Schaer, Mani Erfanian Abdoust, Linus Netze, Meik Bittkowski
- **Abstract**: Retrieval test collections are essential for evaluating information retrieval systems, yet they often lack generalizability across tasks. To overcome this limitation, we introduce REANIMATOR, a versatile framework designed to enable the repurposing of existing test collections by enriching them with extracted and synthetic resources. REANIMATOR enhances test collections from PDF files by parsing full texts and machine-readable tables, as well as related contextual information. It then employs state-of-the-art large language models to produce synthetic relevance labels. Including an optional human-in-the-loop step can help validate the resources that have been extracted and generated. We demonstrate its potential with a revitalized version of the TREC-COVID test collection, showcasing the development of a retrieval-augmented generation system and evaluating the impact of tables on retrieval-augmented generation. REANIMATOR enables the reuse of test collections for new applications, lowering costs and broadening the utility of legacy resources.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces REANIMATOR, a framework for repurposing existing information retrieval test collections by enriching them with extracted and synthetic resources. REANIMATOR extracts full text, tables, captions, and in-text references from PDF files. It then uses large language models (LLMs) to generate synthetic relevance labels for these extracted components. The framework optionally includes a human-in-the-loop step for validation. The authors demonstrate the framework by revitalizing the TREC-COVID test collection (TREC-COVID+), adding tables, context, and synthetic relevance judgments. They evaluate the impact of tables in Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) systems using this revitalized collection.  The paper emphasizes lowering costs and broadening the utility of legacy resources by enabling reuse for new applications like table retrieval and RAG.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of re-animating test collections is not entirely new, as prior work has explored reusing and adapting existing datasets.  However, the specific combination of features in REANIMATOR – automated extraction of diverse modalities (tables, figures, text), synthetic relevance judgment using LLMs *with* the option of human-in-the-loop validation, and a focus on enabling modern IR tasks like RAG – presents a notable advancement. The integration of existing tools (Docling, ir_datasets, UMBRELA) into a coherent framework is also a positive aspect, but reduces the individual novelty of each component. The application to tables is important, given the paucity of table-aware test collections.

*   **Significance:** Test collection development is a costly and time-consuming endeavor. A framework that effectively allows existing resources to be updated and extended to new domains and tasks addresses a practical and important problem. The focus on RAG, a highly active research area, makes this contribution timely.  The creation of TREC-COVID+ and the evaluation of tables in RAG is a valuable contribution as it provides a benchmark that didn't previously exist. Open-sourcing the code and data, as promised, would amplify the impact significantly. The presented experiments, while focused on a specific domain, provides a good starting point for other researchers.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses a real problem: the high cost and limited adaptability of IR test collections.
    *   Provides a comprehensive framework with multiple useful components.
    *   Includes practical evaluation demonstrating the framework's functionality and benefit.
    *   Emphasis on reproducibility and open-source availability.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on LLMs for synthetic relevance judgment raises concerns about the quality and potential bias of these judgments.  The paper acknowledges these concerns but requires stronger validation of the LLM judgments, including more extensive human assessment and inter-annotator agreement measures. Surrogates performed poorly, meaning LLM or human annotators are needed.
    *   While the framework seems flexible, the evaluation focuses heavily on TREC-COVID, limiting the evidence for its generalizability to other types of collections and tasks.
    *   The evaluation of RAG is limited to specific models and parameters. The impact of table extraction and RAG parameter tuning on end performance should be thoroughly evaluated.
    *   The comparison of RAG approaches using different retrieval sources (texts and tables), raises concerns about a valid evaluation, as "the retrieved context originates from distinct information bases, making direct comparisons unreliable." This weakens the RAG performance evaluation.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the framework is effective and easy to use, it could significantly reduce the cost of creating and maintaining test collections, fostering innovation in areas like table retrieval and RAG. The revitalization process should increase efficiency in IR testing. This is especially useful for rapidly changing domains, as demonstrated with COVID-19.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a useful and timely contribution to the field of information retrieval. However, the dependence on LLM-generated relevance judgments and certain weaknesses in the RAG evaluation, together with a relatively narrow experimental focus, limit its immediate impact. While the combination of existing tools is a strength from a practical standpoint, it also detracts from the overall novelty. Stronger validation of the LLM judgments and more extensive evaluation would increase the paper's impact.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Boosting Universal LLM Reward Design through the Heuristic Reward Observation Space Evolution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07596v1)
- **Authors**: Zen Kit Heng, Zimeng Zhao, Tianhao Wu, Yuanfei Wang, Mingdong Wu, Yangang Wang, Hao Dong
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are emerging as promising tools for automated reinforcement learning (RL) reward design, owing to their robust capabilities in commonsense reasoning and code generation. By engaging in dialogues with RL agents, LLMs construct a Reward Observation Space (ROS) by selecting relevant environment states and defining their internal operations. However, existing frameworks have not effectively leveraged historical exploration data or manual task descriptions to iteratively evolve this space. In this paper, we propose a novel heuristic framework that enhances LLM-driven reward design by evolving the ROS through a table-based exploration caching mechanism and a text-code reconciliation strategy. Our framework introduces a state execution table, which tracks the historical usage and success rates of environment states, overcoming the Markovian constraint typically found in LLM dialogues and facilitating more effective exploration. Furthermore, we reconcile user-provided task descriptions with expert-defined success criteria using structured prompts, ensuring alignment in reward design objectives. Comprehensive evaluations on benchmark RL tasks demonstrate the effectiveness and stability of the proposed framework. Code and video demos are available at jingjjjjjie.github.io/LLM2Reward.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework to improve LLM-driven reward design for reinforcement learning (RL). The approach focuses on evolving the Reward Observation Space (ROS) through a heuristic sampling method. Key innovations include:

1.  **State Execution Table:** A mechanism to track historical state usage and success, breaking the Markovian constraint typically found in LLM dialogues. This allows the LLM to explore a broader range of states and avoid getting stuck in local optima.
2.  **Text-Code Reconciliation:** A strategy to align user-provided task descriptions with expert-defined success criteria using structured prompts, which aims to address potential conflicts in objectives.
3.  **Observation Space Disentanglement:** Separate handling of space member selection and internal member operation, reducing complexity and improving comparability.

The framework is evaluated on benchmark RL tasks, demonstrating improved effectiveness and stability compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a genuinely innovative approach to LLM-driven reward design by introducing mechanisms for improved exploration and knowledge grounding. The state execution table is a clever way to address the Markovian constraint and inject memory into the LLM dialogue. The text-code reconciliation tackles a significant issue of potential misalignment between user intent and expert criteria, often overlooked in previous works. The disentanglement of the observation space is also a smart move, streamlining the reward design process.

*   **Significance:** Reward design is a major bottleneck in RL, particularly for complex tasks. The paper has potential to significantly impact the field by making reward engineering more accessible and efficient. Automating reward design has profound implications for sim-to-real transfer and the development of autonomous robotic systems. The work effectively bridges the gap between high-level user intent (natural language) and low-level reward functions required for RL agents.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges in LLM-driven reward design.
    *   **Technically Sound:** The proposed approach is well-motivated and technically solid, with a good blend of LLM prompting and RL principles.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experimental evaluation covers a range of tasks and includes thorough ablation studies, providing strong evidence for the effectiveness of each component.
    *   **Addresses a Real-World Problem:** Reward design is a practical problem that significantly impacts the performance of RL systems. This paper directly addresses this challenge.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the paper shows improvements in reward design, it does not explicitly address the computational cost associated with the LLM interactions and iterative design process. This could be a limiting factor in some applications.
    *   **Task-Specific Thresholds:** While the task-specific threshold is proposed, the improvement is inconsistent across tasks, indicating a need for better tuning or adaptation. Further investigation is needed.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** The paper lacks a rigorous theoretical analysis of the convergence properties of the proposed heuristic approach. While empirical results are strong, a theoretical foundation would strengthen the work.
    *   **Reliance on IsaacGym:** The use of a specific simulator might limit the generalizability of the results to other robotic platforms or environments.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence research in automated reward design, robotic learning, and embodied AI. It offers a practical and effective solution to a crucial problem, and the introduced techniques (state execution table, text-code reconciliation) could be adopted and extended in future work. The method could be applied and extended to different robotic domains.

*   **Comparision of LLM Preprocessing :**
    * For a fair comparison with the existing approaches [13], [32], [33], our LLM preprocessing is isolated from the subsequent design iterations and the reconciled success is still invisible during LLM design.

**Overall:**

The paper represents a significant contribution to the field of automated reward design for RL. The approach is innovative, well-evaluated, and addresses a relevant problem. While there are some limitations, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses, and the paper has the potential to impact the field significantly.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### VLM-R1: A Stable and Generalizable R1-style Large Vision-Language Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07615v1)
- **Authors**: Haozhan Shen, Peng Liu, Jingcheng Li, Chunxin Fang, Yibo Ma, Jiajia Liao, Qiaoli Shen, Zilun Zhang, Kangjia Zhao, Qianqian Zhang, Ruochen Xu, Tiancheng Zhao
- **Abstract**: Recently DeepSeek R1 has shown that reinforcement learning (RL) can substantially improve the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) through a simple yet effective design. The core of R1 lies in its rule-based reward formulation, which leverages tasks with deterministic ground-truth answers to enable precise and stable reward computation. In the visual domain, we similarly observe that a wide range of visual understanding tasks are inherently equipped with well-defined ground-truth annotations. This property makes them naturally compatible with rule-based reward mechanisms. Motivated by this observation, we investigate the extension of R1-style reinforcement learning to Vision-Language Models (VLMs), aiming to enhance their visual reasoning capabilities. To this end, we develop VLM-R1, a dedicated framework designed to harness RL for improving VLMs' performance on general vision-language tasks. Using this framework, we further explore the feasibility of applying RL to visual domain. Experimental results indicate that the RL-based model not only delivers competitive performance on visual understanding tasks but also surpasses Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) in generalization ability. Furthermore, we conduct comprehensive ablation studies that uncover a series of noteworthy insights, including the presence of reward hacking in object detection, the emergence of the "OD aha moment", the impact of training data quality, and the scaling behavior of RL across different model sizes. Through these analyses, we aim to deepen the understanding of how reinforcement learning enhances the capabilities of vision-language models, and we hope our findings and open-source contributions will support continued progress in the vision-language RL community. Our code and model are available at https://github.com/om-ai-lab/VLM-R1
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "VLM-R1: A Stable and Generalizable R1-style Large Vision-Language Model":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VLM-R1, a framework for applying R1-style reinforcement learning (RL) to Vision-Language Models (VLMs).  VLM-R1 aims to improve the visual reasoning capabilities of VLMs on general vision-language tasks. The framework is designed to be flexible, scalable, and easy to use. It leverages the GRPO algorithm, LoRA-based training, multi-node training, supports multi-image inputs, is compatible with various VLMs (QwenVL, InternVL), custom datasets, and mixed modality training.  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of VLM-R1 on Referring Expression Comprehension (REC) and Open-Vocabulary Object Detection (OVD) tasks. Experimental results show that RL improves performance and generalization compared to supervised fine-tuning (SFT). The paper also includes ablation studies that explore reward hacking, the "OD aha moment," the impact of training data quality, and scaling behavior across model sizes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the application and adaptation of the R1-style RL approach to the *general* vision-language domain. While previous works have explored RL for multimodal tasks, this paper attempts to provide a unified and modular framework, VLM-R1, for enabling the application of R1-style RL across various vision-language tasks. The investigation into reward hacking in object detection ("OD aha moment") and the impact of different training datasets also provides valuable insights.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge: improving the visual reasoning abilities of VLMs, particularly in tasks where they underperform compared to specialized vision models. The reported gains in generalization, especially on out-of-domain benchmarks, are significant. The analysis of reward hacking and data dependencies offers practical guidance for training VLMs using RL. The open-source release of the VLM-R1 framework and associated models can facilitate further research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The development of VLM-R1 provides a valuable resource for the community, allowing easier experimentation with RL-based training for VLMs.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments on REC and OVD demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach, with significant improvements over SFT baselines, particularly in generalization ability.
    *   **Detailed Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide insights into the behavior of RL-trained VLMs, including reward hacking and the impact of training data.
    *   **Open Source Release:** Making the code and models publicly available promotes reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited RL Algorithm Support:** The current implementation focuses solely on the GRPO algorithm. While GRPO is a reasonable starting point, future work should explore the integration of other RL algorithms to increase the framework's flexibility and applicability.
    *   **Dependency on Deterministic Ground Truths:** The R1-style approach relies on tasks with well-defined, deterministic ground-truth answers. While this works for REC and OVD, it limits the applicability to tasks where such annotations are unavailable or ambiguous.
    *   **Incremental Improvement over Existing Work:** While providing useful contributions to the field, the novelty and the results is more of an incremental improvement on existing literature such as DeepSeek-R1 (on LM side) and other works on VLMs. The "OD aha moment" effect is a good observation though but it requires further investigation to understand why this emergent behaviour helps boost performance for OVD.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:
    *   Promoting the use of RL for improving VLMs.
    *   Providing a standardized framework for RL-based training.
    *   Inspiring further research into reward design and data selection for RL-trained VLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper makes a solid contribution by providing a framework and analysis for applying R1-style RL to VLMs, the novelty is somewhat incremental, building upon existing work in both language and vision-language modeling. The results are promising, and the insights into reward hacking and data selection are valuable, but the limited RL algorithm support and reliance on deterministic ground truths are limitations. Therefore, the paper receives a **Score: 6**. It is a good, solid paper with positive results and provides valuable insight into the field but has some limitations as mentioned above.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Beating Transformers using Synthetic Cognition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07619v1)
- **Authors**: Alfredo Ibias, Miguel Rodriguez-Galindo, Hector Antona, Guillem Ramirez-Miranda, Enric Guinovart
- **Abstract**: The road to Artificial General Intelligence goes through the generation of episodic reactive behaviors, where the Transformer architecture has been proven to be the state-of-the-art. However, they still fail to develop reasoning. Recently, a novel approach for developing cognitive architectures, called Synthetic Cognition, has been proposed and implemented to develop instantaneous reactive behavior. In this study, we aim to explore the use of Synthetic Cognition to develop episodic reactive behaviors. We propose a mechanism to deal with sequences for the recent implementation of Synthetic Cognition, and test it against DNA foundation models in DNA sequence classification tasks. In our experiments, our proposal clearly outperforms the DNA foundation models, obtaining the best score on more benchmark tasks than the alternatives. Thus, we achieve two goals: expanding Synthetic Cognition to deal with sequences, and beating the Transformer architecture for sequence classification.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores using Synthetic Cognition (SC), a novel approach for developing cognitive architectures, to handle episodic reactive behaviors, specifically DNA sequence classification.  The authors propose a method to adapt SC, initially designed for instantaneous inputs, to handle sequential data by employing a sliding window approach, effectively treating each sequence as a series of time-stamped inputs. This modified SC is then tested against established DNA foundation models (Transformer-based) on a benchmark of 44 DNA sequence classification tasks. The experiments demonstrate that the proposed SC approach outperforms the Transformer-based models in a greater number of datasets, even without the extensive pre-training required by the Transformer models. This highlights SC's potential for sequence classification and as a primitive-based framework for building more complex cognitive architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the application and adaptation of Synthetic Cognition to sequence data. While sliding windows are a common technique, their integration within the SC framework for this specific task (DNA sequence classification) presents a new perspective. The paper challenges the dominance of Transformer-based models in sequence processing by offering an alternative, representation-based approach that doesn't rely on weight tuning via backpropagation and gradient descent. The results also point to the fact that this method does not need pre-training, which provides an advantage in terms of computational resources compared to Transformer models.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from several factors. Firstly, demonstrating SC's effectiveness in sequence classification, a domain currently dominated by Transformers, positions it as a viable alternative. Secondly, the lack of reliance on pre-training offers a potential advantage in terms of computational efficiency and resource consumption, especially for smaller datasets or resource-constrained environments. Thirdly, the paper strengthens the SC framework as a general building block for cognitive architectures, demonstrating its adaptability to handle temporal data. The paper further outlines some shortcomings and ways to address them, demonstrating that the authors have analyzed their method well.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clearly defines the problem and motivation.
    *   Provides a good overview of related work (Transformer-based DNA foundation models).
    *   The approach is relatively simple to implement.
    *   The experimental results are convincing, demonstrating SC's superior performance on several datasets.
